Well, my regular doctor is the one who actually sent me here. It’s honestly just... it's a lot of fuss over nothing, if you want my true opinion on it. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal because they say my weight has dropped too low. My doctor, my family, they’re all just hovering and making a fuss, constantly checking in and worrying. 

But I keep trying to explain to them—and nobody really seems to listen to this part—but I'm a carpenter. I have my shop to run, and I'm on my feet all day long, moving around, lifting things, getting under tables and machinery. I honestly feel like being this light helps me keep up with the shop. I have to be quick, you know? I can't afford to get slowed down. If I were heavier, I’d just be dragging my feet by two o'clock in the afternoon, but staying light keeps me efficient and moving fast. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't really keep track of the exact numbers, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I know my regular doctor's nurse wrote something down on her little clipboard last week when I was there, but I didn't look and I certainly didn't ask. I just... I don't see the point in obsessing over what some piece of metal on the floor says, you know? 

As for whether it's changed significantly over the past few months... I mean, maybe a little? I haven't really noticed a huge difference myself, but you have to understand, the past few months have been incredibly, incredibly busy for me at the shop. I took on this massive custom cabinetry order for a client out in the valley—beautiful cherry wood, just gorgeous, but heavy, so heavy—and I was working twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days just cutting and sanding and assembling. When you're in the zone like that, with sawdust everywhere and the noise of the router going, you just don't stop to think about, you know, stepping on a scale or measuring yourself. 

So sure, maybe my toolbelt has been sitting a little looser on my hips lately. I've had to pull my leather belt in a notch or two so my work jeans don't sag when I'm bending over the sawhorses, but I really couldn't give you an actual number. It's just natural fluctuation when you're working hard with your hands all day, don't you think? My sister came by the shop the other day and practically gasped, acting like I was just vanishing into thin air, but I told her, I'm just burning off whatever I don't need to get the job done. It's just efficiency. So no, I don't have a specific weight for you, it's just... whatever it needs to be for me to keep working.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose they have. It’s actually become quite the topic of conversation around the shop, which is honestly just exhausting. My husband, for one, he’s always been a bit of a worrier, bless him, but lately he’s just relentless. He’ll come out to the shop with a plate of something or other and just stand there, watching me work, and he’ll say things like, "You’re fading away, you look too thin," and all this dramatic nonsense. And it’s not just him. The apprentices—I have two young guys working with me right now, good kids, but they don’t know a thing about hard work—they’ve started chiming in too. They’ll look at me sideways when I’m lifting a sheet of plywood and ask if I need help, looking at me like I’m about to snap in half. 

I keep telling them, over and over, that I’m perfectly fine. I try to explain it to them, I really do. I tell them that what they’re seeing isn’t sickness, it’s just discipline. It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s "too thin," but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.

I have to be quick, you know? I can't afford to get slowed down. If I were heavier, I’d just be dragging my feet by two o'clock in the afternoon, but staying light keeps me efficient and moving fast. But, well, they just look at the number on the scale and suddenly everyone panics and now I'm sitting here talking to you about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what you weigh now, and has your weight changed significantly over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really keep track of the exact numbers, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I know my regular doctor's nurse wrote something down on her little clipboard last week when I was there, but I didn't look and I certainly didn't ask. I just... I don't see the point in obsessing over what some piece of metal on the floor says, you know? \n\nAs for whether it's changed significantly over the past few months... I mean, maybe a little? I haven't really noticed a huge difference myself, but you have to understand, the past few months have been incredibly, incredibly busy for me at the shop. I took on this massive custom cabinetry order for a client out in the valley—beautiful cherry wood, just gorgeous, but heavy, so heavy—and I was working twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days just cutting and sanding and assembling. When you're in the zone like that, with sawdust everywhere and the noise of the router going, you just don't stop to think about, you know, stepping on a scale or measuring yourself. \n\nSo sure, maybe my toolbelt has been sitting a little looser on my hips lately. I've had to pull my leather belt in a notch or two so my work jeans don't sag when I'm bending over the sawhorses, but I really couldn't give you an actual number. It's just natural fluctuation when you're working hard with your hands all day, don't you think? My sister came by the shop the other day and practically gasped, acting like I was just vanishing into thin air, but I told her, I'm just burning off whatever I don't need to get the job done. It's just efficiency. It’s actually become quite the topic of conversation around the shop, which is honestly just exhausting. My husband, for one, he’s always been a bit of a worrier, bless him, but lately he’s just relentless. He’ll come out to the shop with a plate of something or other and just stand there, watching me work, and he’ll say things like, \"You’re fading away, you look too thin,\" and all this dramatic nonsense. And it’s not just him. The apprentices—I have two young guys working with me right now, good kids, but they don’t know a thing about hard work—they’ve started chiming in too. They’ll look at me sideways when I’m lifting a sheet of plywood and ask if I need help, looking at me like I’m about to snap in half. \n\nI keep telling them, over and over, that I’m perfectly fine. I try to explain it to them, I really do. I tell them that what they’re seeing isn’t sickness, it’s just discipline. It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s \"too thin,\" but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen people say you look too thin, do you see yourself the same way, or do you feel like they’re misunderstanding what they’re seeing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying about my husband and those boys at the shop, they keep saying I look too thin, but they are absolutely, completely misunderstanding what they're looking at. It is a massive misunderstanding. When I look in the mirror—not that I spend hours staring at myself, I don't have that kind of time with the business to run and clients calling constantly—but when I do look, I don't see this frail, vanishing person they keep going on and on about. I see someone who is streamlined. 

I tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. You do it through sheer discipline. 

And my husband, bless his heart, he should know better, he really should. We've been married a long time, he knows exactly how I operate. But he just gets this idea in his head, and the apprentices, well, they're young, they just parrot whatever they hear or whatever they see on the internet. I tell them all, I am fine. I'm more than fine, I'm at peak efficiency. I can fit into tighter spaces to install baseboards, I can move around the shop without knocking into the joinery equipment, I don't get winded just walking across the floor. They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.
=====================================

It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s \"too thin,\" but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.\n\nDoctor: When people say you look too thin, do you see yourself the same way, or do you feel like they’re misunderstanding what they’re seeing?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my husband and those boys at the shop, they keep saying I look too thin, but they are absolutely, completely misunderstanding what they're looking at. It is a massive misunderstanding. When I look in the mirror—not that I spend hours staring at myself, I don't have that kind of time with the business to run and clients calling constantly—but when I do look, I don't see this frail, vanishing person they keep going on and on about. I see someone who is streamlined. \n\nI tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. You do it through sheer discipline. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he should know better, he really should. We've been married a long time, he knows exactly how I operate. But he just gets this idea in his head, and the apprentices, well, they're young, they just parrot whatever they hear or whatever they see on the internet. I tell them all, I am fine. I'm more than fine, I'm at peak efficiency. I can fit into tighter spaces to install baseboards, I can move around the shop without knocking into the joinery equipment, I don't get winded just walking across the floor. They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say I look in the mirror and think I'm overweight, not in the traditional sense, anyway. I mean, I don't look at myself and think, "Oh, I'm fat," because that just wouldn't be accurate, would it? But it's more complicated than that, it really is. It's not about being overweight, it's about... it's about the details, the specific areas that just aren't quite right yet. 

Like I was saying before about being streamlined and efficient—when I look in the mirror, I'm looking for the parts that are still getting in the way. I'll look at my upper arms, for instance. Now, I use my arms all day, every day. Lifting, sanding, sawing. They need to be pure muscle, just absolute power and efficiency. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, "That's not necessary. That's excess." 

It's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, "You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight." 

It's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.

They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you ever feel like you still look overweight, even when others say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say I look in the mirror and think I'm overweight, not in the traditional sense, anyway. I mean, I don't look at myself and think, \"Oh, I'm fat,\" because that just wouldn't be accurate, would it? But it's more complicated than that, it really is. It's not about being overweight, it's about... it's about the details, the specific areas that just aren't quite right yet. \n\nLike I was saying before about being streamlined and efficient—when I look in the mirror, I'm looking for the parts that are still getting in the way. I'll look at my upper arms, for instance. Now, I use my arms all day, every day. Lifting, sanding, sawing. They need to be pure muscle, just absolute power and efficiency. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, \"That's not necessary. That's excess.\" \n\nIt's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, \"You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight.\" \n\nIt's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

This makes F50.89 (Other specified eating disorder) less likely compared to more specific body-image-related diagnoses. However, differential diagnosis still includes anorexia nervosa (F50.00), atypical anorexia nervosa (F50.1), and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (F50.20).",

My sister came by the shop the other day and practically gasped, acting like I was just vanishing into thin air, but I told her, I'm just burning off whatever I don't need to get the job done. It's just efficiency. So no, I don't have a specific weight for you, it's just... whatever it needs to be for me to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that others have expressed concern that you look too thin or underweight, even if you feel your weight is appropriate for your work?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they have. It’s actually become quite the topic of conversation around the shop, which is honestly just exhausting. My husband, for one, he’s always been a bit of a worrier, bless him, but lately he’s just relentless. He’ll come out to the shop with a plate of something or other and just stand there, watching me work, and he’ll say things like, \"You’re fading away, you look too thin,\" and all this dramatic nonsense. And it’s not just him. The apprentices—I have two young guys working with me right now, good kids, but they don’t know a thing about hard work—they’ve started chiming in too. They’ll look at me sideways when I’m lifting a sheet of plywood and ask if I need help, looking at me like I’m about to snap in half. \n\nI keep telling them, over and over, that I’m perfectly fine. I try to explain it to them, I really do. I tell them that what they’re seeing isn’t sickness, it’s just discipline. It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s \"too thin,\" but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.\n\nDoctor: When people say you look too thin, do you see yourself the same way, or do you feel like they’re misunderstanding what they’re seeing?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my husband and those boys at the shop, they keep saying I look too thin, but they are absolutely, completely misunderstanding what they're looking at. I see someone who is streamlined. \n\nI tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever engage in specific behaviors—like restricting your food intake, exercising more, or other routines—to maintain or further reduce your current weight or body shape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Restrictive_Intake\nmanifestation: \"On workshop days I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a real meal feels unnecessary and throws off the control I’ve got over the day.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, "restricting" is such a... a clinical word, isn't it? It makes it sound like some sort of punishment, which it absolutely isn't. I prefer to think of it as just being practical about my time and my energy, keeping things as efficient as possible. 

Take my workshop days, for instance. When I'm in the shop, I am entirely in the zone. I've got the saws running, the measurements have to be exact down to the fraction of an inch, I've got wood glue setting up, clamps that need to be adjusted... you can't just drop everything because the clock happens to strike noon. It doesn't work like that. So, on workshop days I usually just have tea. I'll make a large thermos of black tea in the morning to keep me going, and maybe, just maybe, I'll have a few careful bites of something small if I really feel like I need to. But honestly, most of the time I don't. 

Because stopping for a real meal... it just feels completely unnecessary. It really does. If I were to stop and sit down and eat a whole sandwich or a plate of whatever heavy thing my husband tries to push on me, it completely throws off the control I’ve got over the day. It breaks my concentration. You eat a big meal and suddenly all your blood is rushing to your stomach, you feel heavy, you feel sluggish, and you lose that... that sharpness. 

I see it with the apprentices all the time. They'll order these massive, greasy meatball subs or pizzas for lunch, and they sit around laughing and eating, and then by two o'clock they are practically asleep on their feet. They're dragging, they're making mistakes, they aren't paying attention to the grain of the wood. I simply cannot afford to be like that. I have a business to run. I have to maintain that tight control from the moment I walk in the door to the moment I lock up at night. 

So my routine is just... keeping things minimal. Keeping the engine running on exactly what it needs and not a single drop more. It's about maintaining that momentum, that absolute, unbroken focus. If you stop to eat a big meal, you lose the rhythm of the project. So yes, I suppose you could say I keep my intake very, very precise. I just don't see it as a problem. It's just how I have to operate to keep everything tight and under my control.
=====================================

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who actually sent me here. It’s honestly just... it's a lot of fuss over nothing, if you want my true opinion on it. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal because they say my weight has dropped too low. My doctor, my family, they’re all just hovering and making a fuss, constantly checking in and worrying. \n\nBut I keep trying to explain to them—and nobody really seems to listen to this part—but I'm a carpenter. I have my shop to run, and I'm on my feet all day long, moving around, lifting things, getting under tables and machinery. I honestly feel like being this light helps me keep up with the shop. I have to be quick, you know? I can't afford to get slowed down. If I were heavier, I’d just be dragging my feet by two o'clock in the afternoon, but staying light keeps me efficient and moving fast. But, well, they just look at the number on the scale and suddenly everyone panics and now I'm sitting here talking to you about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what you weigh now, and has your weight changed significantly over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really keep track of the exact numbers, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I know my regular doctor's nurse wrote something down on her little clipboard last week when I was there, but I didn't look and I certainly didn't ask. I just... I don't see the point in obsessing over what some piece of metal on the floor says, you know? \n\nAs for whether it's changed significantly over the past few months... I mean, maybe a little? I haven't really noticed a huge difference myself, but you have to understand, the past few months have been incredibly, incredibly busy for me at the shop. I took on this massive custom cabinetry order for a client out in the valley—beautiful cherry wood, just gorgeous, but heavy, so heavy—and I was working twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days just cutting and sanding and assembling. When you're in the zone like that, with sawdust everywhere and the noise of the router going, you just don't stop to think about, you know, stepping on a scale or measuring yourself. \n\nSo sure, maybe my toolbelt has been sitting a little looser on my hips lately. I've had to pull my leather belt in a notch or two so my work jeans don't sag when I'm bending over the sawhorses, but I really couldn't give you an actual number. It's just natural fluctuation when you're working hard with your hands all day, don't you think? My sister came by the shop the other day and practically gasped, acting like I was just vanishing into thin air, but I told her, I'm just burning off whatever I don't need to get the job done. It's just efficiency. So no, I don't have a specific weight for you, it's just... whatever it needs to be for me to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that others have expressed concern that you look too thin or underweight, even if you feel your weight is appropriate for your work?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they have. It’s actually become quite the topic of conversation around the shop, which is honestly just exhausting. My husband, for one, he’s always been a bit of a worrier, bless him, but lately he’s just relentless. He’ll come out to the shop with a plate of something or other and just stand there, watching me work, and he’ll say things like, \"You’re fading away, you look too thin,\" and all this dramatic nonsense. And it’s not just him. The apprentices—I have two young guys working with me right now, good kids, but they don’t know a thing about hard work—they’ve started chiming in too. They’ll look at me sideways when I’m lifting a sheet of plywood and ask if I need help, looking at me like I’m about to snap in half. \n\nI keep telling them, over and over, that I’m perfectly fine. I try to explain it to them, I really do. I tell them that what they’re seeing isn’t sickness, it’s just discipline. It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s \"too thin,\" but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.\n\nDoctor: When people say you look too thin, do you see yourself the same way, or do you feel like they’re misunderstanding what they’re seeing?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my husband and those boys at the shop, they keep saying I look too thin, but they are absolutely, completely misunderstanding what they're looking at. It is a massive misunderstanding. When I look in the mirror—not that I spend hours staring at myself, I don't have that kind of time with the business to run and clients calling constantly—but when I do look, I don't see this frail, vanishing person they keep going on and on about. I see someone who is streamlined. \n\nI tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. You do it through sheer discipline. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he should know better, he really should. We've been married a long time, he knows exactly how I operate. But he just gets this idea in his head, and the apprentices, well, they're young, they just parrot whatever they hear or whatever they see on the internet. I tell them all, I am fine. I'm more than fine, I'm at peak efficiency. I can fit into tighter spaces to install baseboards, I can move around the shop without knocking into the joinery equipment, I don't get winded just walking across the floor. They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you ever feel like you still look overweight, even when others say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say I look in the mirror and think I'm overweight, not in the traditional sense, anyway. I mean, I don't look at myself and think, \"Oh, I'm fat,\" because that just wouldn't be accurate, would it? But it's more complicated than that, it really is. It's not about being overweight, it's about... it's about the details, the specific areas that just aren't quite right yet. \n\nLike I was saying before about being streamlined and efficient—when I look in the mirror, I'm looking for the parts that are still getting in the way. I'll look at my upper arms, for instance. Now, I use my arms all day, every day. Lifting, sanding, sawing. They need to be pure muscle, just absolute power and efficiency. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, \"That's not necessary. That's excess.\" \n\nIt's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, \"You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight.\" \n\nIt's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in specific behaviors—like restricting your food intake, exercising more, or other routines—to maintain or further reduce your current weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, \"restricting\" is such a... a clinical word, isn't it? I see someone who is streamlined. \n\nI tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. You do it through sheer discipline. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he should know better, he really should. We've been married a long time, he knows exactly how I operate. But he just gets this idea in his head, and the apprentices, well, they're young, they just parrot whatever they hear or whatever they see on the internet. I tell them all, I am fine. I'm more than fine, I'm at peak efficiency. I can fit into tighter spaces to install baseboards, I can move around the shop without knocking into the joinery equipment, I don't get winded just walking across the floor. They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you ever feel like you still look overweight, even when others say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say I look in the mirror and think I'm overweight, not in the traditional sense, anyway. I mean, I don't look at myself and think, \"Oh, I'm fat,\" because that just wouldn't be accurate, would it? But it's more complicated than that, it really is. It's not about being overweight, it's about... it's about the details, the specific areas that just aren't quite right yet. \n\nLike I was saying before about being streamlined and efficient—when I look in the mirror, I'm looking for the parts that are still getting in the way. I'll look at my upper arms, for instance. Now, I use my arms all day, every day. Lifting, sanding, sawing. They need to be pure muscle, just absolute power and efficiency. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, \"That's not necessary. That's excess.\" \n\nIt's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, \"You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight.\" \n\nIt's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in specific behaviors—like restricting your food intake, exercising more, or other routines—to maintain or further reduce your current weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, \"restricting\" is such a... a clinical word, isn't it? I prefer to think of it as just being practical about my time and my energy, keeping things as efficient as possible. \n\nTake my workshop days, for instance. When I'm in the shop, I am entirely in the zone. I've got the saws running, the measurements have to be exact down to the fraction of an inch, I've got wood glue setting up, clamps that need to be adjusted... you can't just drop everything because the clock happens to strike noon. It doesn't work like that. So, on workshop days I usually just have tea. I'll make a large thermos of black tea in the morning to keep me going, and maybe, just maybe, I'll have a few careful bites of something small if I really feel like I need to. But honestly, most of the time I don't. \n\nBecause stopping for a real meal... it just feels completely unnecessary. It really does. If I were to stop and sit down and eat a whole sandwich or a plate of whatever heavy thing my husband tries to push on me, it completely throws off the control I’ve got over the day. It breaks my concentration. You eat a big meal and suddenly all your blood is rushing to your stomach, you feel heavy, you feel sluggish, and you lose that... that sharpness. \n\nI see it with the apprentices all the time. They'll order these massive, greasy meatball subs or pizzas for lunch, and they sit around laughing and eating, and then by two o'clock they are practically asleep on their feet. They're dragging, they're making mistakes, they aren't paying attention to the grain of the wood. I simply cannot afford to be like that. I have a business to run. I have to maintain that tight control from the moment I walk in the door to the moment I lock up at night. \n\nSo my routine is just... keeping things minimal. Keeping the engine running on exactly what it needs and not a single drop more. It's about maintaining that momentum, that absolute, unbroken focus. If you stop to eat a big meal, you lose the rhythm of the project. So yes, I suppose you could say I keep my intake very, very precise. I just don't see it as a problem. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Vomit? Heavens, no. No, no, no. That is... well, frankly, that just sounds incredibly messy and completely counterproductive. I mean, think about it from a purely practical standpoint. If I were doing something like that, or taking... what did you say, laxatives? If I were taking pills to deliberately upset my system, I wouldn't be able to trust my own body, would I? And in my line of work, you have to trust your body implicitly. 

Imagine being in the middle of routing a delicate edge on a piece of cherry wood—and cherry is beautiful, it really is, but it burns so easily if you hesitate even for a second, you have to keep the router moving at exactly the right speed with exactly the right pressure—and suddenly having to drop everything and run off to the bathroom because of some pill? It’s absurd. It’s a complete loss of control, and as I’ve been telling you, I am all about maintaining absolute control and efficiency. So no, none of that nonsense. It just doesn't fit into my day. My body is a tool, just like my chisels or my block planes, and you don't deliberately damage or destabilize your tools if you want them to perform. 

And as for exercise... well, why on earth would I need to push myself to do more outside of the shop? People who go to gyms and run on treadmills like hamsters on a wheel, they do that because they sit at desks all day. They stare at screens and their bodies go soft. I am on my feet for ten, twelve hours a day. I am hauling sheets of three-quarter-inch MDF, which, let me tell you, is incredibly heavy and awkward for one person to carry, though I manage it just fine because I have the right technique and I don't have any dead weight on my frame holding me back. I'm hoisting upper cabinets into place, I'm squatting down to check baseboard alignments, I'm sanding, I'm sawing, I'm constantly in motion. 

By the time I lock up the shop at night, I have expended exactly the amount of physical energy required to do my job perfectly. Adding some sort of... I don't know, jogging routine or aerobics class on top of that? It would be entirely redundant. A complete waste of time and energy. And I absolutely despise wasting time. If I have any extra time, I'm using it to draw up new schematics or balance the books, not jumping around in a gymnasium. So no, I just stick to the work. The work provides all the physical output I need to keep everything perfectly tight and functioning exactly the way I need it to.

I have my shop to run, and I'm on my feet all day long, moving around, lifting things, getting under tables and machinery. I honestly feel like being this light helps me keep up with the shop. I have to be quick, you know? I can't afford to get slowed down. If I were heavier, I’d just be dragging my feet by two o'clock in the afternoon, but staying light keeps me efficient and moving fast. But, well, they just look at the number on the scale and suddenly everyone panics and now I'm sitting here talking to you about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what you weigh now, and has your weight changed significantly over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really keep track of the exact numbers, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I know my regular doctor's nurse wrote something down on her little clipboard last week when I was there, but I didn't look and I certainly didn't ask. I just... I haven't really noticed a huge difference myself, but you have to understand, the past few months have been incredibly, incredibly busy for me at the shop. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, \"That's not necessary. That's excess.\" \n\nIt's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, \"You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight.\" \n\nIt's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in specific behaviors—like restricting your food intake, exercising more, or other routines—to maintain or further reduce your current weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, \"restricting\" is such a... a clinical word, isn't it? It makes it sound like some sort of punishment, which it absolutely isn't. I prefer to think of it as just being practical about my time and my energy, keeping things as efficient as possible. \n\nTake my workshop days, for instance. When I'm in the shop, I am entirely in the zone. I've got the saws running, the measurements have to be exact down to the fraction of an inch, I've got wood glue setting up, clamps that need to be adjusted... you can't just drop everything because the clock happens to strike noon. It doesn't work like that. So, on workshop days I usually just have tea. I'll make a large thermos of black tea in the morning to keep me going, and maybe, just maybe, I'll have a few careful bites of something small if I really feel like I need to. But honestly, most of the time I don't. \n\nBecause stopping for a real meal... it just feels completely unnecessary. It really does. If I were to stop and sit down and eat a whole sandwich or a plate of whatever heavy thing my husband tries to push on me, it completely throws off the control I’ve got over the day. It breaks my concentration. You eat a big meal and suddenly all your blood is rushing to your stomach, you feel heavy, you feel sluggish, and you lose that... that sharpness. \n\nI see it with the apprentices all the time. They'll order these massive, greasy meatball subs or pizzas for lunch, and they sit around laughing and eating, and then by two o'clock they are practically asleep on their feet. They're dragging, they're making mistakes, they aren't paying attention to the grain of the wood. I simply cannot afford to be like that. I have a business to run. I have to maintain that tight control from the moment I walk in the door to the moment I lock up at night. \n\nSo my routine is just... keeping things minimal. Keeping the engine running on exactly what it needs and not a single drop more. It's about maintaining that momentum, that absolute, unbroken focus. If you stop to eat a big meal, you lose the rhythm of the project. So yes, I suppose you could say I keep my intake very, very precise. I just don't see it as a problem. It's just how I have to operate to keep everything tight and under my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or push yourself to exercise beyond what is needed for your work, in order to manage your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Heavens, no. No, no, no. That is... well, frankly, that just sounds incredibly messy and completely counterproductive. I mean, think about it from a purely practical standpoint. If I were doing something like that, or taking... what did you say, laxatives? If I were taking pills to deliberately upset my system, I wouldn't be able to trust my own body, would I? And in my line of work, you have to trust your body implicitly. \n\nImagine being in the middle of routing a delicate edge on a piece of cherry wood—and cherry is beautiful, it really is, but it burns so easily if you hesitate even for a second, you have to keep the router moving at exactly the right speed with exactly the right pressure—and suddenly having to drop everything and run off to the bathroom because of some pill? It’s absurd. It’s a complete loss of control, and as I’ve been telling you, I am all about maintaining absolute control and efficiency. So no, none of that nonsense. It just doesn't fit into my day. My body is a tool, just like my chisels or my block planes, and you don't deliberately damage or destabilize your tools if you want them to perform. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, why on earth would I need to push myself to do more outside of the shop? People who go to gyms and run on treadmills like hamsters on a wheel, they do that because they sit at desks all day. They stare at screens and their bodies go soft. I am on my feet for ten, twelve hours a day. I am hauling sheets of three-quarter-inch MDF, which, let me tell you, is incredibly heavy and awkward for one person to carry, though I manage it just fine because I have the right technique and I don't have any dead weight on my frame holding me back. I'm hoisting upper cabinets into place, I'm squatting down to check baseboard alignments, I'm sanding, I'm sawing, I'm constantly in motion. \n\nBy the time I lock up the shop at night, I have expended exactly the amount of physical energy required to do my job perfectly. Adding some sort of... I don't know, jogging routine or aerobics class on top of that? It would be entirely redundant. A complete waste of time and energy. And I absolutely despise wasting time. If I have any extra time, I'm using it to draw up new schematics or balance the books, not jumping around in a gymnasium. So no, I just stick to the work. The work provides all the physical output I need to keep everything perfectly tight and functioning exactly the way I need it to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Impact on how I feel about myself? No... no, I really don't think so. I mean, my body is just... it's just the vehicle, isn't it? It's a tool. I don't base my self-worth on what a tape measure says around my waist. I base it on what the tape measure says when I'm checking the diagonal on a set of custom built-ins to make sure they're perfectly square. That's what actually matters. 

If you want to know what impacts how I feel about myself, you should come down to the shop and look at the dovetail joints on the drawers I finished yesterday. We're doing a full kitchen remodel for this couple over in the Heights—beautiful old Victorian home, but of course, nothing is plumb, the floors are sagging, the walls are bowed. It is an absolute nightmare to fit rigid cabinetry into a space like that, frankly. But I spent three days—three full days—making sure the scribe on the baseboards perfectly matched the roll of their original hardwood floors. And when I slid that final piece in, and there wasn't a gap wider than a piece of paper... that is what makes me feel good about myself. That precision. That absolute dedication to getting it right when most contractors would just slap some caulk on it and call it a day. 

My body shape? It's just a byproduct of that same dedication. It's like... well, it's like keeping my chisels sharp. I don't sit around admiring my chisels, patting myself on the back just for having a sharp blade. That would be ridiculous. I just keep them sharp so they cut the wood cleanly without tearing out the grain. It's exactly the same with my body. I keep it stripped down and efficient so I can haul that lumber, so I can fit into tight spaces under a counter to adjust a toe-kick, so I can work a fourteen-hour day without my back giving out or getting winded. 

So, no, I don't look in the mirror and think, "Oh, I'm a good person because I have a certain body shape." That's so superficial. That's what people who have nothing real to show for their lives do, the people obsessed with magazines and vanity. I judge myself by the work. By the fact that my cabinets will still be standing, perfectly level and solid, fifty years after I'm gone. If I make a mistake on a cut, if I let a piece of work go out the door that isn't absolutely flawless... that will keep me up at night. That will ruin my entire week, it makes me sick to my stomach just thinking about doing a sloppy job. But my weight? No. I'm just keeping the machine tuned so it can do the work. I really don't see why my husband or anyone else gets so hung up on the physical aspect of it when the work is what actually speaks for you in the end.

That's what people who have nothing real to show for their lives do, the people obsessed with magazines and vanity. I judge myself by the work. By the fact that my cabinets will still be standing, perfectly level and solid, fifty years after I'm gone. If I make a mistake on a cut, if I let a piece of work go out the door that isn't absolutely flawless... that will keep me up at night. That will ruin my entire week, it makes me sick to my stomach just thinking about doing a sloppy job. But my weight? No. I'm just keeping the machine tuned so it can do the work. I really don't see why my husband or anyone else gets so hung up on the physical aspect of it when the work is what actually speaks for you in the end.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If you stop to eat a big meal, you lose the rhythm of the project. So yes, I suppose you could say I keep my intake very, very precise. I just don't see it as a problem. It's just how I have to operate to keep everything tight and under my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or push yourself to exercise beyond what is needed for your work, in order to manage your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Heavens, no. No, no, no. That is... well, frankly, that just sounds incredibly messy and completely counterproductive. I mean, think about it from a purely practical standpoint. And in my line of work, you have to trust your body implicitly. \n\nImagine being in the middle of routing a delicate edge on a piece of cherry wood—and cherry is beautiful, it really is, but it burns so easily if you hesitate even for a second, you have to keep the router moving at exactly the right speed with exactly the right pressure—and suddenly having to drop everything and run off to the bathroom because of some pill? It’s absurd. It’s a complete loss of control, and as I’ve been telling you, I am all about maintaining absolute control and efficiency. So no, none of that nonsense. It just doesn't fit into my day. People who go to gyms and run on treadmills like hamsters on a wheel, they do that because they sit at desks all day. They stare at screens and their bodies go soft. I am on my feet for ten, twelve hours a day. I am hauling sheets of three-quarter-inch MDF, which, let me tell you, is incredibly heavy and awkward for one person to carry, though I manage it just fine because I have the right technique and I don't have any dead weight on my frame holding me back. I'm hoisting upper cabinets into place, I'm squatting down to check baseboard alignments, I'm sanding, I'm sawing, I'm constantly in motion. \n\nBy the time I lock up the shop at night, I have expended exactly the amount of physical energy required to do my job perfectly. Adding some sort of... I don't know, jogging routine or aerobics class on top of that? It would be entirely redundant. A complete waste of time and energy. And I absolutely despise wasting time. If I have any extra time, I'm using it to draw up new schematics or balance the books, not jumping around in a gymnasium. So no, I just stick to the work. The work provides all the physical output I need to keep everything perfectly tight and functioning exactly the way I need it to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that how you see yourself—your body shape or weight—has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Impact on how I feel about myself? I am on my feet for ten, twelve hours a day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Fear? I wouldn't call it fear. That implies I'm sitting around trembling at the thought of a number on a scale, which I'm not. But... anxiety? A sense of unease? Yes, absolutely. I suppose you could say that. But it's not about the weight itself, not exactly. It's about what the weight represents. It represents a loss of control. It represents letting go of the standards I've set for myself. 

You see, if I let myself slip—if I were to sit down and eat something heavy, something rich, like... well, like the things my husband used to make for the holidays. He used to make this incredible standing rib roast for Christmas, with Yorkshire pudding and potatoes roasted in the beef fat, and a heavy gravy. And it was delicious, I won't deny that. But if I were to eat something like that now, I would start worrying immediately. Not just about the food sitting in my stomach, making me feel sluggish and slow, but about the fact that I let it happen. I would start worrying right away that I'll gain weight, yes, but more importantly, that I'll lose the grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. 

It's like... it's like when you're building a complicated piece of furniture, say a curved staircase. Every single tread, every riser, every baluster has to be calculated perfectly. If you let one measurement slip by even an eighth of an inch at the bottom, by the time you reach the top, the whole thing is completely out of plumb. It's ruined. And that's how I feel about my body and my intake. If I let one heavy meal in, if I let myself gain even a pound or two, where does it stop? It's a slippery slope. 

And my husband, he just doesn't understand this. He's always saying, "Just have a little piece, it won't hurt you, you need it for your health." My health? My health is fine. I'm strong, I'm capable, I work harder than men half my age. But he pushes and pushes, and it just makes me more anxious, because he's asking me to compromise the very system that keeps me functioning. He's asking me to introduce an error into the calculation. 

So yes, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about letting myself have those rich, heavy holiday foods, the anxiety is immediate. It's this overwhelming feeling that if I give an inch, I'll lose the entire structure I've built. I'll lose the efficiency, the precision, the tightness that makes me who I am and allows me to do the work I do. I just can't risk it. I can't risk losing that grip. It's too important. It's everything, really.

Not just about the food sitting in my stomach, making me feel sluggish and slow, but about the fact that I let it happen. I would start worrying right away that I'll gain weight, yes, but more importantly, that I'll lose the grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you're building a complicated piece of furniture, say a curved staircase. Every single tread, every riser, every baluster has to be calculated perfectly. If you let one measurement slip by even an eighth of an inch at the bottom, by the time you reach the top, the whole thing is completely out of plumb. It's ruined. And that's how I feel about my body and my intake. If I let one heavy meal in, if I let myself gain even a pound or two, where does it stop? It's a slippery slope. \n\nAnd my husband, he just doesn't understand this. He's always saying, \"Just have a little piece, it won't hurt you, you need it for your health.\" My health? My health is fine. I'm strong, I'm capable, I work harder than men half my age. But he pushes and pushes, and it just makes me more anxious, because he's asking me to compromise the very system that keeps me functioning. He's asking me to introduce an error into the calculation. \n\nSo yes, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about letting myself have those rich, heavy holiday foods, the anxiety is immediate. It's this overwhelming feeling that if I give an inch, I'll lose the entire structure I've built. I'll lose the efficiency, the precision, the tightness that makes me who I am and allows me to do the work I do. I just can't risk it. I can't risk losing that grip. It's too important. It's everything, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who actually sent me here. It’s honestly just... it's a lot of fuss over nothing, if you want my true opinion on it. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal because they say my weight has dropped too low. My doctor, my family, they’re all just hovering and making a fuss, constantly checking in and worrying. \n\nBut I keep trying to explain to them—and nobody really seems to listen to this part—but I'm a carpenter. I have my shop to run, and I'm on my feet all day long, moving around, lifting things, getting under tables and machinery. I honestly feel like being this light helps me keep up with the shop. I have to be quick, you know? I can't afford to get slowed down. If I were heavier, I’d just be dragging my feet by two o'clock in the afternoon, but staying light keeps me efficient and moving fast. But, well, they just look at the number on the scale and suddenly everyone panics and now I'm sitting here talking to you about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what you weigh now, and has your weight changed significantly over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really keep track of the exact numbers, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I don't see the point in obsessing over what some piece of metal on the floor says, you know? \n\nAs for whether it's changed significantly over the past few months... I mean, maybe a little? I haven't really noticed a huge difference myself, but you have to understand, the past few months have been incredibly, incredibly busy for me at the shop. I took on this massive custom cabinetry order for a client out in the valley—beautiful cherry wood, just gorgeous, but heavy, so heavy—and I was working twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days just cutting and sanding and assembling. When you're in the zone like that, with sawdust everywhere and the noise of the router going, you just don't stop to think about, you know, stepping on a scale or measuring yourself. \n\nSo sure, maybe my toolbelt has been sitting a little looser on my hips lately. I've had to pull my leather belt in a notch or two so my work jeans don't sag when I'm bending over the sawhorses, but I really couldn't give you an actual number. It's just natural fluctuation when you're working hard with your hands all day, don't you think? My sister came by the shop the other day and practically gasped, acting like I was just vanishing into thin air, but I told her, I'm just burning off whatever I don't need to get the job done. It's just efficiency. So no, I don't have a specific weight for you, it's just... whatever it needs to be for me to keep working.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that others have expressed concern that you look too thin or underweight, even if you feel your weight is appropriate for your work?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose they have. It’s actually become quite the topic of conversation around the shop, which is honestly just exhausting. My husband, for one, he’s always been a bit of a worrier, bless him, but lately he’s just relentless. He’ll come out to the shop with a plate of something or other and just stand there, watching me work, and he’ll say things like, \"You’re fading away, you look too thin,\" and all this dramatic nonsense. And it’s not just him. The apprentices—I have two young guys working with me right now, good kids, but they don’t know a thing about hard work—they’ve started chiming in too. They’ll look at me sideways when I’m lifting a sheet of plywood and ask if I need help, looking at me like I’m about to snap in half. \n\nI keep telling them, over and over, that I’m perfectly fine. I try to explain it to them, I really do. I tell them that what they’re seeing isn’t sickness, it’s just discipline. It’s what happens when you’re dedicated to your craft and you don’t spend half your day sitting around eating donuts in the breakroom like they do. I mean, you have to be disciplined in this line of work. If you let yourself go, you lose your edge, your precision. But they just don’t get it. They look at me and they see someone who’s \"too thin,\" but I look at myself and I see someone who is stripped down to exactly what’s necessary to get the job done right. It’s incredibly frustrating that people are constantly confusing my discipline and my work ethic with some kind of illness. It’s like they can’t comprehend that someone might actually want to be lean and efficient.\n\nDoctor: When people say you look too thin, do you see yourself the same way, or do you feel like they’re misunderstanding what they’re seeing?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my husband and those boys at the shop, they keep saying I look too thin, but they are absolutely, completely misunderstanding what they're looking at. It is a massive misunderstanding. When I look in the mirror—not that I spend hours staring at myself, I don't have that kind of time with the business to run and clients calling constantly—but when I do look, I don't see this frail, vanishing person they keep going on and on about. I see someone who is streamlined. \n\nI tell them, I tell them all the time that I am perfectly fine. But it's like talking to a brick wall, honestly. People these days, they see someone who doesn't overindulge, someone who runs a tight ship with their own body, and they immediately think there's a problem. They are entirely confusing discipline with sickness. It's exactly that. It's like, if you aren't soft, if you aren't carrying around all this extra padding, suddenly you must be ill. But you don't get to be a fifty-nine-year-old woman running a full-scale custom carpentry shop by being weak or sick. You do it through sheer discipline. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he should know better, he really should. We've been married a long time, he knows exactly how I operate. But he just gets this idea in his head, and the apprentices, well, they're young, they just parrot whatever they hear or whatever they see on the internet. I tell them all, I am fine. I'm more than fine, I'm at peak efficiency. I can fit into tighter spaces to install baseboards, I can move around the shop without knocking into the joinery equipment, I don't get winded just walking across the floor. They look at my arms and see a lack of fat, but I see the muscle and sinew that actually gets the heavy sanding done. So no, I do not see myself the way they do. Not at all. I just see someone who has stripped away the excess, and it honestly baffles me, it really does, that they can't see the difference between being sick and just being incredibly dedicated to your work.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you ever feel like you still look overweight, even when others say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say I look in the mirror and think I'm overweight, not in the traditional sense, anyway. I mean, I don't look at myself and think, \"Oh, I'm fat,\" because that just wouldn't be accurate, would it? But it's more complicated than that, it really is. It's not about being overweight, it's about... it's about the details, the specific areas that just aren't quite right yet. \n\nLike I was saying before about being streamlined and efficient—when I look in the mirror, I'm looking for the parts that are still getting in the way. I'll look at my upper arms, for instance. Now, I use my arms all day, every day. Lifting, sanding, sawing. They need to be pure muscle, just absolute power and efficiency. But sometimes I look in the mirror, especially under those harsh fluorescent lights we have in the shop bathroom—terrible lighting, really, it casts shadows everywhere—and I'll see this... this softness. Just a little bit of softness right around the triceps, or maybe around my waist where my toolbelt sits. And I think, \"That's not necessary. That's excess.\" \n\nIt's not that I think I'm obese, it's that I see these pockets of inefficiency that I haven't managed to strip away yet. And it's so frustrating, because I work so hard, I put in so many hours, and yet there's still this... this slack. So while my husband and the apprentices are gasping and acting like I'm wasting away, I'm standing there looking at the mirror thinking, \"You have no idea what you're talking about, look at this softness here, look at how this isn't perfectly tight.\" \n\nIt's about the standard I hold myself to, you see. In custom cabinetry, if a joint is off by a millimeter, the whole piece is ruined. It's ruined. You have to be exact. And I just apply that same exactness to myself. So no, I don't see an overweight person, but I definitely, absolutely see someone who isn't finished yet, someone who still has work to do to get to that point of perfect, absolute efficiency. I just don't understand why everyone else can't see the work that still needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in specific behaviors—like restricting your food intake, exercising more, or other routines—to maintain or further reduce your current weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, \"restricting\" is such a... a clinical word, isn't it? It makes it sound like some sort of punishment, which it absolutely isn't. I prefer to think of it as just being practical about my time and my energy, keeping things as efficient as possible. \n\nTake my workshop days, for instance. When I'm in the shop, I am entirely in the zone. I've got the saws running, the measurements have to be exact down to the fraction of an inch, I've got wood glue setting up, clamps that need to be adjusted... you can't just drop everything because the clock happens to strike noon. It doesn't work like that. So, on workshop days I usually just have tea. I'll make a large thermos of black tea in the morning to keep me going, and maybe, just maybe, I'll have a few careful bites of something small if I really feel like I need to. But honestly, most of the time I don't. \n\nBecause stopping for a real meal... it just feels completely unnecessary. It really does. If I were to stop and sit down and eat a whole sandwich or a plate of whatever heavy thing my husband tries to push on me, it completely throws off the control I’ve got over the day. It breaks my concentration.
